C. 
Platform Service Revenues (“PSRs”) 
18 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing any PSRs?  
19 
A 
Yes.  The Companies propose two PSRs for revenues from: (i) the E-
20 
Commerce Platform; and (ii) the UESC program.       
21 
141


--- Page 142 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
117 of 120 
 
Q. 
Please describe the E-Commerce Platform PSR. 
1 
A. 
Consistent with the REV Track Two Order and the 2017 NMPC Rate Case 
2 
Order, the Companies propose a PSR sharing mechanism for fees 
3 
collected by the Companies from vendors who participate in the E-
4 
Commerce Platform.  During the Rate Year and each Data Year, the 
5 
Companies propose to retain 20 percent of the fees, deferring the 
6 
remaining 80 percent for future credit to customers.  An example of this 
7 
mechanism is set forth in Exhibit ___(FOH-9). 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the UESC program PSR. 
10 
A. 
During the Rate Year and each of the Data Years, the Companies 
11 
propose to retain one-third of the fees received from UESC participants, 
12 
deferring the remaining two-thirds for future credit to customers.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Why do the Companies believe the UESC sharing mechanism is 
15 
reasonable? 
16 
A. 
For the UESC program, unlike the E-Commerce Platform, the 
17 
Companies are undertaking additional marketing and feasibility 
18 
management responsibilities without a guarantee that those efforts will 
19 
result in a contract.  Because the PSR would only apply if the initial 
20 
marketing and feasibility study activities result in customers entering a 
21 
142


--- Page 143 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
118 of 120 
 
UESC with the Companies and moving forward with the improvements 
1 
identified in the feasibility study, the Companies believe a higher sharing 
2 
percentage is reasonable.  It establishes a meaningful incentive for the 
3 
Companies to continue to grow the UESC program, while also rewarding 
4 
customers for the contributions toward marketing and the feasibility 
5 
studies. 
6 
 
7 
 
D. 
Non-Pipeline Alternatives (“NPA”) Incentive Mechanism 
8 
Q. 
Do the Companies proposing an NPA Incentive Mechanism? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  In support of the NPA proposal discussed in KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
10 
GIOPs’ testimonies, and consistent with the “Report of Niagara Mohawk 
11 
Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid Concerning the Non-Pipeline 
12 
Alternatives Incentive Mechanism Collaborative” filed December 21, 
13 
2018 in Case 17-G-0239, the Companies propose a mechanism for each 
14 
company to share 30 percent of the difference between the costs of a 
15 
traditional investment and the proposed cost of an NPA, adjusted for other 
16 
net benefits.  This sharing mechanism follows the basic structure of the 
17 
non-wires alternative (“NWA”) mechanism approved by the Commission 
18 
in the 2017 NMPC Rate Case. The proposed NPA incentive mechanism is 
19 
set forth in Exhibit ___ (FOH-11). 
20 
21 
143


--- Page 144 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
119 of 120 
 
E. 
KEDLI EmPower Replacement Program Incentive Mechanism 
1 
Q. 
Is KEDLI proposing to continue the EmPower Replacement Program 
2 
Incentive Mechanism? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Commission 
4 
approved an earnings incentive mechanism for the EmPower Replacement 
5 
Program, incentivizing KEDLI to achieve cost savings and promote 
6 
program participation.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
How is the incentive mechanism structured? 
9 
A. 
The incentive amount is determined using a percentage of the approved 
10 
program budget, excluding labor costs, and scaled so that each tier of the 
11 
program design has a return that reflects the value of participation in that 
12 
tier.  KEDLI is not proposing any modifications to the existing structure of 
13 
the earnings incentive mechanism.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How will the incentive mechanism be calculated? 
16 
A. 
KEDLI proposes the earnings incentive mechanism be awarded annually 
17 
after all committed projects have been completed and no encumbered 
18 
funds remain.  For each tier, KEDLI recommends applying the same 
19 
percentages established in the 2016 Rate Case to the annual budget value 
20 
for calculating the earnings incentive per household served.  The 
21 
144


--- Page 145 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
120 of 120 
 
percentages are 0.007 percent for Tier Three and 0.014 percent for Tier 
1 
Four.  The earnings incentive amount per household served is then 
2 
multiplied by participation in each tier, resulting in a total incentive award 
3 
to the company.  Exhibit ___ (FOH-12) includes an example of the 
4 
earnings incentive mechanism. 
5

3 
A. 
Reducing Methane Emissions  
4 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendations regarding the Companies’ 
5 
proposed methane reduction initiatives.   
6 
A. 
The Staff Pipeline Safety Panel opposes several of the Companies’ 
7 
initiatives aimed at reducing methane emissions from the gas distribution 
8 
system, including eliminating the current leak prone pipe (“LPP”) 
9 
removal incentive, as well as the current incentive for repairing high-
10 
emitting Type 3 leaks.  Removing LPP and repairing high emitting leaks 
11 
are particularly effective means of reducing methane emissions from the 
12 
gas system.  Given the safety, reliability, and environmental benefits 
13 
associated with eliminating system leaks, the Companies should be 
14 
incented to drive continued improvements in this area through 
15 
challenging but fair incentives that align with the Companies’ ambitious 
16 
target to reduce methane emissions from the gas distribution system 60 
17 
percent (from 1990 levels) by 2035.  Staff’s opposition to these 
18 
incentives is counter to the Commission’s stated policies for eliminating 
19 
159


--- Page 160 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 5 of 66 
 
LPP and lowering emissions.  The Companies’ Gas Safety Panel further 
1 
addresses these incentive mechanisms.  
2

Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation regarding the level of ETIP 
5

The Staff Efficiency and Sustainability Panel (“SESP”) recommends (pp. 
7 
20-22) using the Companies’ proposed energy efficiency budgets as 
8 
placeholders until the Commission sets budgets in the on-going statewide 
9 
proceeding in Case 18-M-0084; and if Commission does not act in Case 
10 
18-M-0084 before rates are set for KEDNY and KEDLI in this case, the 
11

12 
amounts determined here and amounts set in the other case.

14 
The Companies disagree with the SESP’s recommendation.  Establishing 
15 
the budgets in this case will provide the Companies with the certainty 
16 
needed to develop and implement their energy efficiency programs over 
17 
the next several years.  Therefore, the SESP’s recommendation should be 
18 
rejected.

2. 
Utility Energy Services Contracts (“UESC”) 
15 
Program  
16 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ position regarding Staff’s recommendation 
17 
on the UESC contracts program?  
18 
A. 
The SESP (pp. 36-38) proposed two modifications to the Companies’ 
19 
proposal: (i) reducing the Companies’ share of net revenues generated 
20 
from UESC program from 33.3 to 20 percent; and (ii) eliminating the two 
21 
164


--- Page 165 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 10 of 66 
 
incremental FTEs supported in the Companies’ case.  As described later in 
1 
our testimony in the section on platform service revenues, the Companies 
2 
disagree with Staff’s proposal to reduce the Companies’ share of platform 
3 
service revenues.  Likewise, the Companies do not accept Staff’s proposal 
4 
to eliminate the two FTE positions.  The Commission has directed utilities 
5 
in the state to identify opportunities to generate platform service revenues 
6 
through the provision of value-added services to customers and 
7 
stakeholders.  The UESC program presents an opportunity for the 
8 
Companies to implement this Commission directive.  To have a 
9 
reasonable chance to succeed, however, the program requires a dedicated 
10 
set of resources.  Requiring that the Companies redeploy resources from 
11 
their energy efficiency programs—which are also charged with 
12 
significantly increasing the penetration of those programs—would result 
13 
in inadequate resources dedicated to both of these important areas.  
14 
Accordingly, the Companies do not agree with Staff’s recommendation to 
15 
eliminate the two incremental FTEs to support the UESC program.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please address the Staff’s proposal that the Companies should be 
18 
required to develop specific energy efficiency targets and budgets for 
19 
UESC program and incorporate the UESC program into their overall 
20 
165


--- Page 166 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 11 of 66 
 
energy efficiency portfolios, to be funded from authorized energy 
1 
efficiency budgets.   
2 
A. 
The Companies do not agree that the UESC program should be 
3 
incorporated into the energy efficiency portfolios and likewise do not 
4 
agree that specific budgets and targets should be established.  The UESC 
5 
program is designed as an energy services contracting program rather than 
6 
as a direct customer incentive program.  Energy savings delivered through 
7 
UESC programs are tracked and measured through the existing utility 
8 
energy efficiency programs as customers participating in the UESC 
9 
program have eligible measures installed.  Therefore, any energy savings 
10 
targets established for UESC would be duplicative of targets already 
11 
established under the existing commercial and industrial energy efficiency 
12 
programs.   
13 
 
14 
Further, the scope of the feasibility studies conducted under the UESC 
15 
program is not limited to gas savings, but also includes electric energy 
16 
savings as well as water savings, operations and maintenance cost savings, 
17 
and support integration of other distributed energy resources.  
18 
Accordingly, the Companies do not support incorporating the UESC 
19 
program into the existing gas-only energy efficiency portfolios.  Rather, 
20 
this program supports broader energy opportunities in line with expanded 
21 
166


--- Page 167 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 12 of 66 
 
customer tools and resources and therefore requires its own set of 
1 
resources and should not be rolled into the Companies’ overall energy 
2 
efficiency portfolios.

3. 
Fuel-Switching Calculator  
4 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendations regarding the Companies’ 
5 
proposed fuel switching calculator.  
6 
A. 
The Companies’ do not agree with the SESP’s recommendation (pp. 38-
7 
40) that approval of the Fuel-Switching Calculator be subject to the costs 
8 
being allocated at the outset to other National Grid companies, or that such 
9 
allocation be based on National Grid’s general allocator (8.29 percent 
10 
KEDLI; 13.31 percent KEDNY).   
11 
 
12 
 
First, the purpose of the calculator would be to enable customers to make 
13 
informed energy choices by using location-specific assumptions – e.g., 
14 
incentives available and rates specific to KEDNY and KEDLI – to 
15 
compare the cost and emissions estimates for different energy alternatives.  
16 
Initially, the fuel-switching calculator would be used solely by KEDNY 
17 
and KEDLI for their customers.  Expanding the calculator for use by other 
18 
National Grid companies will add to the costs of the fuel switching 
19 
calculator (a fact Staff does not reflect in its proposed adjustment).  To the 
20 
extent the calculator is modified for use by other National Grid companies 
21 
167


--- Page 168 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 13 of 66 
 
in the future, the costs of those modifications will be reflected, and the 
1 
cost allocations would be revised in accordance with National Grid’s 
2 
established allocation process for National Grid Service Company costs.  
3 
Accordingly, the Companies do not agree with the recommendation that 
4 
approval of the calculator be conditioned on the initial costs being initially 
5 
charged to other National Grid companies. 
6 
 
7 
 
In addition, when other National Grid companies begin using the 
8 
calculator, National Grid Service Company will apply the appropriate 
9 
allocator based on the benefitting companies at that time.  The general 
10 
allocator proposed by Staff, which allocates costs among all National Grid 
11 
companies (including electric-only companies and National Grid Service 
12 
Company), would not be the appropriate allocator for the costs of the fuel-
13 
switching calculator.

A. 
The Companies strongly disagree with the SESP’s recommendation that 
1 
the Commission reject the proposed expansion of the Geothermal 
2 
Demonstration Project.  The 2016 Gas REV Demonstration Project 
3 
(“Riverhead Demonstration”), which ends December 2019, has allowed 
4 
KEDLI to provide non-traditional heating and cooling services to 
5 
participating customers since November 2017.  Since then, the Riverhead 
6 
Demonstration has shown this technology works and customers can 
7 
achieve savings.  As stated in the Panel’s direct testimony (p. 35), it is 
8 
estimated each geothermal customer could realize annual energy savings 
9 
between $1,000 and $1,500.  The expanded geothermal demonstration 
10 
program proposes to develop and test a geothermal product that will be a 
11 
cost-effective alternative to a customer’s existing heating solution.  The 
12 
Riverhead Demonstration also confirmed the underground portion is a 
13 
significant market barrier towards wider adoption of geothermal.  Exhibit 
14 
__ (FOH-2R) is a copy of KEDLI’s Q3 2018 demonstration project status 
15 
report, wherein it shows actual energy savings of 31 percent to 58 
16 
percent, which is $229.10 to $684.95 in per-customer savings from just 
17 
January 2018 to May 2018 (much of the 2018 Heating Season).   
18 
 
19 
The proposed expanded Geothermal Demonstration Proposal is designed 
20 
to test that the business model of utility ownership of underground loops, 
21

the most expensive part of the system, can accelerate greater market 
1

3 
to entry for all customers but especially for low-to-moderate income, 
4

5

G. 
Reducing Carbon Emissions on the Gas Network 
9 
1. 
Renewable Natural Gas (“RNG”) 
10 
a) 
Newtown Creek Project  
11 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation (SPP pp. 39-57) with respect 
12 
to the Newtown Creek Project.  
13 
A. 
The Panel disagrees with Staff’s proposed adjustment regarding the 
14 
Newtown Creek Project.  Staff’s agreed that KEDNY should proceed with 
15 
the project as part of the Company’s last rate cases (Case 16-G-0059).  
16 
The adjustment Staff proposes here would discourage the Companies, and 
17 
other utilities, from pursuing innovative solutions to address GHG 
18 
emissions.  As stated previously, climate change is an issue that requires 
19 
action now, and decisions that discourage innovative approaches in favor 
20 
181


--- Page 182 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 27 of 66 
 
of the status quo can be detrimental.  Further, even assuming the Company 
1 
agreed to an adjustment as Staff proposes, Staff erred in how it calculated 
2 
and applied the value of the adjustment.   
3 
 
4 
The basis for Staff’s downward adjustment and capping cost recovery 
5 
based on revenues generated is that the cost of gas produced by the project 
6 
is higher than the Company’s weighted average cost of gas.  The project 
7 
was not intended to produce RNG at the cost of traditional natural gas.  
8 
Rather, the project was intended to help meet New York City’s 
9 
environmental goals while also demonstrating a new source of renewable 
10 
energy, and how energy providers and other stakeholders can collaborate 
11 
to incorporate RNG into the supply portfolio to meet sustainability goals, 
12 
enhance reliability and utilize an existing energy network to meet the 
13 
energy demands of New York City. 
14 
 
15 
The fact that the unit cost of RNG is greater today than conventionally 
16 
produced gas is not surprising and is reminiscent of similar comparisons 
17 
between wind and solar electric renewables to fossil-fired generation 
18 
sources a decade ago.  Just as the cost disparity between renewable and 
19 
fossil-fired electric generation has shifted with time, investments need to 
20 
be made now to encourage the scaling of RNG to achieve cost savings.  It 
21 
182


--- Page 183 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 28 of 66 
 
is imperative that the economy move to a much greater share of clean 
1 
energy sources.  Indeed, the CLCPA mentioned earlier requires that the 
2 
state move to 70 percent renewable generation by 2030 and that New York 
3 
State achieve an 85 percent reduction in GHG emissions by 2050.  
4 
Fulfilling this mandate will require innovation and the Company believes 
5 
it will require decarbonization of the gas network through the development 
6 
of RNG.  It is the Panel’s position that, for RNG to become more 
7 
prevalent and for the costs of RNG to approach parity with conventionally 
8 
produced resources as renewable and conventional electricity sources 
9 
have, there must be regulatory support for investments such as the 
10 
Newtown Creek project.  
11 
 
12 
The benefits of the Newtown Creek project extend far beyond the average 
13 
cost of the RNG produced.  For example, Exhibit __(FOH-3R), is a copy 
14 
of 2017 presentation delivered by the American Gas Association’s Chief 
15 
Regulatory Counsel at a national EPA Gas STAR conference that 
16 
highlights the Newtown Creek project as a case study for utilities’ RNG 
17 
interconnections, and for the potential of public-private partnerships to 
18 
reduce metropolitan GHG emissions.  To focus on the unit cost of the gas 
19 
produced and injected into the system ignores those other important 
20 
183


--- Page 184 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 29 of 66 
 
benefits.  To make progress on the ambitious clean energy targets, the 
1 
industry must innovate. 
2 
 
3 
It should also be noted that this project was developed in collaboration 
4 
with the City of New York at one of the most complex wastewater 
5 
treatment plants in the country creating a set of unique challenges that 
6 
escalated the cost of this project over time.  These challenges include 
7 
obtaining the approval of Public Design Commission to ensure that the 
8 
purification facility was aligned with the Newtown Creek masterplan and 
9 
its overall architecture.  The project site also had to be moved after 
10 
detailed engineering assessment highlighted a risk that was unforeseen in 
11 
the preliminary engineering analysis. 
12 
   
13 
Q. 
On what other basis does the Panel disagree with Staff’s adjustment 
14 
to the Newtown Creek Project?   
15 
A. 
Even if KEDNY agreed with the basis for Staff’s adjustment, Staff erred 
16 
in how it calculated and applied that adjustment for two reasons.  First, 
17 
Staff intended to increase the project’s capital costs based on the current 
18 
estimate of $37.898 million.  However, Staff included the entire $37.898 
19 
million estimate amount as a fiscal year 2020 plant addition on Exhibit 
20 
__ (SPP-3), but did not take into consideration the Company’s fiscal year 
21 
184


--- Page 185 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 30 of 66 
 
2021 capital addition of $0.051 million in Exhibit __ (SPP-3).  As a 
1 
result, Staff overstated the estimated project cost in Exhibit __ (SPP-3) 
2 
by $0.051 million.   
3 
 
4 
Second, Staff’s intent was to impute a revenue adjustment on the revenue 
5 
requirement of the Newtown Creek project, which is the rationale behind 
6 
their calculation including the Company’s current project estimate of 
7 
$37.898 million.  However, Staff failed to increase their revenue 
8 
requirement in Exhibit __ (SSP-3) to use the same $37.898 million 
9 
project estimate.  Staff confirmed this oversight in response to IR NG-13, 
10 
a copy of which is included as Exhibit __ (FOH-4R).  As a result, the 
11 
Company is revising the Newtown Creek project estimate in its capital 
12

13 
estimate of $37.898 million, consistent with Staff’s revised Exhibit __ 
14 
(SGIOP-4).   
15 
 
16 
b) 
P2G Demonstration Project (“P2G Project”) 
17 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation regarding the P2G Project.   
18 
A. 
The SESP recommends the proposal be rejected because it is not 
19 
identified as one of the “envisioned solutions … for excess renewable 
20 
electricity” in the Commission’s December 12, 2018 Order Establishing 
21 
185


--- Page 186 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 31 of 66 
 
Energy Storage Goal and Deployment Policy in Case 18-E-0130 
1 
(“Storage Order”).  Staff’s basis for rejecting the proposal is contrary to 
2 
the spirit of innovation necessary for the state to achieve its emissions 
3 
reductions objectives and contrary to the intent of the Storage Order.  
4 
  
The Storage Order set a target of 1500 MW of energy storage 
5 
deployment by 2025 and an aspirational deployment goal of 3000 MW 
6 
energy storage statewide by 2030.  It did not preclude technology types 
7 
and neither does Public Service Law Section 74, which compelled the 
8 
Commission to develop targets and policies.  The law expressly identifies 
9 
a range of storage types for various purposes, including the use of 
10 
chemical reactions to store energy in usable forms by defining a 
11 
“qualified energy storage system” as one which is “commercially 
12 
available technology that is capable of absorbing energy, storing it for a 
13 
period of time, and thereafter dispatching the energy using mechanical, 
14 
chemical, or thermal processes to store energy that was generated at one 
15 
time for use at a later time.”  PSL §74 (emphasis added). 
16 
 
17 
Even if one reads PSL §74 and the Storage Order narrowly to only target 
18 
storage related to the electric system, the P2G demonstration meets this 
19 
criteria.  Adding net carbon neutral gas to the gas distribution system can 
20 
promote a cleaner electric system by reducing gas system constraints and 
21 
186


--- Page 187 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 32 of 66 
 
thus allowing RNG to displace the combustion of dirtier fuels.  Further, by 
1 
providing an additional end use market for carbon-free electric generation, 
2 
P2G may stimulate the development of additional renewable generation 
3 
resources. 
4 
 
5 
Further, as the Commission has articulated, the Energy Storage Roadmap 
6 
“is technology-neutral and acknowledges that a range of energy storage 
7 
solutions will be deployed to best meet customer and system needs.”  
8 
(Storage Order p. 5).   
9 
 
10 
Finally, as the Panel has mentioned previously, the CLCPA is the most 
11 
progressive decarbonization legislation in the United States and to achieve 
12 
the state’s decarbonization mandates, the Companies must enable 
13 
innovation and facilitate the addition of new technologies and business 
14 
models.  If commercialized at scale, P2G has the potential to significantly 
15 
decarbonize the gas network.  This project will provide insight into how to 
16 
integrate such resources into the gas network and streamline the 
17 
interconnection process for this new gas source.  
18 
 
19 
Accordingly, the Panel disagrees with Staff’s rationale for opposing the 
20 
P2G Demonstration Project.   
21 
187


--- Page 188 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 33 of 66 
 
c) 
Hydrogen Blending Study 
1 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation regarding the Companies’ 
2 
proposed Hydrogen Blending project.   
3 
A. 
Similar to the P2G Demonstration project, the SESP opposes (pp. 59-60) 
4 
the proposed Hydrogen Blending study because hydrogen blending is not 
5 
a specific solution identified in the Commission’s Storage Order.  The 
6 
Companies disagree with Staff’s position. 
7 
 
8 
The Companies’ primary interest in hydrogen is as an additional potential 
9 
pathway toward decarbonization of the gas network.  Hydrogen 
10 
production is not a function of biomass and waste feedstock availability, 
11 
and thus hydrogen blending could play a significant role in expanding the 
12 
potential of low carbon gas and achieving the state’s deep 
13 
decarbonization goals.  In terms of cost and customer experience, 
14 
hydrogen blending may be the least disruptive way to decarbonize the 
15 
gas network.  Through the hydrogen blending study, the Companies seek 
16 
to understand what actions will be required to prepare gas system 
17 
infrastructure for hydrogen. 
18 
 
19 
188


--- Page 189 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 34 of 66 
 
The proposed hydrogen blending study would provide necessary 
1 
information for the development of a hydrogen readiness plan, focusing on 
2 
safety, reliability, and operability of the Companies’ specific network. 
3 
d) 
RNG Interconnections and Future of Heat 
4 
Engineering Group. 
5 
Q.

6 
proposed RNG Interconnections program.

8

9

13

14 
the adjustments to remove KEDNY’s associated Rate Year operating and 
15 
capital expenditures under the program.  
16 
 
17 
The Companies’ RNG interconnection queue has increased significantly 
18 
over the past few years, growing from zero to six proposals within 
19 
KEDNY and KEDLI.  Additional resources are needed to support the 
20 
development of this local and renewable supply of gas.  The Companies’ 
21 
189


--- Page 190 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 35 of 66 
 
proposal is designed to alleviate two barriers to RNG interconnection, the 
1 
extensive time it takes for a developer to receive interconnection approval 
2 
and the high capital cost of the interconnection. 
3 
 
4 
The time it takes to complete the interconnection approval process is a 
5 
barrier to the development of RNG projects. The additional FTEs 
6 
requested for the program would be responsible for processing RNG 
7 
interconnection proposals, which would reduce the time to evaluate 
8 
requests.  The Companies currently rely on existing gas engineering 
9 
personnel to support the evaluation of interconnection proposals and 
10 
conduct feasibility analyses and interconnection design.  These existing 
11 
FTEs are dedicated to supporting the Companies’ traditional gas 
12 
engineering activities, and RNG interconnection processing is additive to 
13 
their existing workload.  As a result, interconnection processing has been 
14 
delayed.  RNG developers rely on receiving timely interconnection 
15 
approval to solidify project financing.  Thus, delays in interconnection 
16 
processing inhibits RNG development.  For these reasons, and as is stated 
17 
in the Companies’ GIOP rebuttal testimony, the incremental FTEs 
18 
included in the FOH Engineering Group are necessary even if the capital 
19 
RNG Interconnection Program were to be deferred by a year.   
20 
 
21 
190


--- Page 191 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 36 of 66 
 
The cost of RNG interconnections has been identified as a key barrier to 
1 
commercializing RNG.  Much of the cost of interconnection is due to 
2 
equipment required by gas utilities, including meters, odorization systems 
3 
and gas quality analyzers. Under the current process, project developers 
4 
are responsible for the cost of this equipment, while the equipment itself is 
5 
maintained and operated by the utility.  Project developers in the 
6 
Companies’ interconnection queue have indicated that this upfront cost is 
7 
prohibitive to the development of their proposed projects.  The 
8 
Companies’ proposed RNG Interconnection program would unburden 
9 
project developers from bearing the cost of equipment that is core to utility 
10 
functions - i.e., equipment required to verify gas quality and quantity 
11 
before it is received into the gas network. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
 Do the Companies’ agree with Staff’s position that FTEs can be 
14 
reallocated from other portions of the gas business?  
15 
A. 
No.  First, Staff (SESP p. 57) suggests that the RNG program is 
16 
insufficiently mature to warrant dedicated FTE resources, and also 
17 
proposes the potential reallocation of FTEs associated with the Clean 
18 
Conversion Program.  As described above, the RNG program is growing 
19 
and accelerating the pace of RNG implementation is an important element 
20 
in decarbonizing the energy system.  Further, contrary to Staff’s 
21 
191


--- Page 192 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 37 of 66 
 
suggestion, the previous elimination of the Clean Conversion Program 
1 
also eliminates any funding associated with the labor resources needed to 
2 
deliver that program.  It would be a double count to propose that the Clean 
3 
Conversion Program not be funded, and that the labor resources associated 
4 
with that unfunded program be used to deliver the RNG Interconnections 
5 
program.  In addition, even if incremental resources were available within 
6 
the Companies, it is unlikely such resources would be immediately 
7 
qualified to perform the work needed of the Future of Heat Engineering

Group. 
9 
 
10 
As the Companies work to meet heating needs through a decarbonized gas 
11 
supply and achieve aggressive clean energy goals using NPAs and RNG 
12

H. 
Performance-Based Incentives and Revenue Sharing 
1 
1. 
EAMs  
2 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Staff EAM Panel’s recommendations 
3 
regarding the Companies’ proposed package of EAMs? 
4 
A. 
No.  The SEAMP makes four overall recommendations (pp. 13-17) 
5 
regarding the Companies’ EAM proposals:  
6 
(i) 
reject KEDNY’s proposal to earn up to 78 basis points and 
7 
KEDLI’s proposal to earn up to 77 basis points in the Rate Year at 
8 
maximum achievement levels and replace those with a program 
9 
allowing each company to earn up to 10 basis points maximum, 
10 
plus earnings from a Share the Savings energy efficiency metric;  
11 
(ii) 
impose more aggressive performance measures on the Companies; 
12 
(iii) 
express the eligible EAM amounts in terms of absolute dollars 
13 
rather than as basis points; and 
14 
(4) 
in the event a multi-year rate plan is adopted, establish EAM 
15 
metrics, targets and financial incentive levels for the full term of 
16 
the rate plan.   
17 
 
Staff also recommends (SEAMP p. 19) that EAMs that may be established 
18 
in this case be “reconsidered as part of a collaborative among the 
19 
Companies, Staff, and other stakeholders” if the Commission authorizes 
20 
new programs or significantly increased budgets as part of a statewide 
21 
193


--- Page 194 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 39 of 66 
 
proceeding.  However, Staff proposes that the number and value of basis 
1 
points available should remain as set in this case.   
2 
     
3 
Q, 
What is the Panel’s position regarding the SEAMP’s proposal to 
4 
replace the Companies’ proposed package of EAMs with Staff’s two 
5 
proposed EAMs? 
6 
A. 
The Panel generally disagrees with Staff’s proposal.  Staff’s EAM 
7 
proposal is most effectively illustrated in Exhibit __ (SEAMP-2), which 
8 
lists six proposed EAM metrics each for KEDNY and KEDLI, with 
9 
potential aggregate maximum values in the Rate Year of 78 and 77 basis 
10 
points, respectively, compared to Staff’s proposals of two EAMs each for 
11 
KEDNY and KEDLI, with maximum values in the Rate Year of 25 basis 
12 
points.  Staff’s proposed EAM maximum values are significantly below 
13 
what the Companies proposed and would not produce the incentives 
14 
necessary to encourage desired outcomes.   
15 
 
16 
In the Track Two Order (Case 14-M-0101, Order Adopting a Ratemaking 
17 
and Utility Revenue Model Policy Framework (May 19, 2016)), the 
18 
Commission stated that incentive mechanisms should provide utilities with 
19 
stretch targets that are reasonably and realistically achievable with 
20 
significant utility efforts and with financially meaningful incentives. Thus, 
21 
194


--- Page 195 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 40 of 66 
 
the Commission selected 100 basis points as the reasonable and 
1 
appropriate ceiling for EAMs. The potentially achievable maximum 
2 
incentive levels proposed by Staff are only one-quarter this level and less 
3 
that one-third what the Companies proposed.  That level of incentive 
4 
would not serve as the mechanism the Commission envisioned. 
5 
 
6 
Further, although there may be merit to a system peak reduction metric, 
7 
the Companies question whether the capabilities are in place to effectively 
8 
implement and administer such a metric by the Rate Year.  Likewise, 
9 
while a Share the Savings metric may have benefits, the Companies do not 
10 
support adopting such a metric to the exclusion of all other EAMs.   
11 
 
 
12 
 
The Companies’ positions regarding the SEAMP’s specific application of 
13 
its overall recommendations are presented below.   
14 
a) 
System Efficiency 
 
15 
(1) 
Peak Reduction 
16 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation regarding the Peak Reduction 
17 
metric of the System Efficiency EAM.   
18 
A. 
The Companies agree an EAM metric designed to encourage the reduction 
19 
of gas peak demand would be beneficial, and support the Commission’s 
20 
preference for an outcome-based EAM.  However, current capabilities are 
21 
195


--- Page 196 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 41 of 66 
 
insufficient to adequately measure peak reduction on a system-level and 
1 
there are limitations on the ability to adequately measure peak reduction at 
2 
a system-level or isolate it from other large macro-economic variables.  
3 
Similar concerns regarding Staff’s proposed outcome-based gas peak 
4 
reduction EAM were raised in the recent Consolidated Edison gas case 
5 
(Case 19-G-0066) (Customer Energy Solutions Update and Rebuttal 
6 
Testimony, p. 219).   
7 
 
8 
 
The Companies are open to working with Staff and other stakeholders to 
9 
try to develop a system-level peak reduction methodology; however, until 
10 
a more robust peak reduction metric can be developed and implemented, 
11 
the Commission should approve the peak-reduction metric proposed by 
12 
the Companies’ in direct testimony (as corrected in the Panel’s 
13 
Corrections & Updates filing).    A programmatic-based metric would 
14 
push the Companies to continue to drive peak reduction, while the 
15 
capabilities needed to allow for system-wide peak reduction measurements 
16 
are developed. 
17 
(2) 
Supply Diversification 
18 
Q. 
Please address Staff’s recommendation regarding the Supply 
19

20 
196


--- Page 197 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 42 of 66 
 
A.  
Staff (SEAMP p. 28) opposes the Supply Diversification metric on the 
1 
basis that the Commission’s support for fuel and resource diversity was 
2 
intended in the context of diversifying the electric grid away from reliance 
3 
on gas, and the Track Two Order did not identify diversification of fuel 
4 
sources within a gas utility’s supply portfolio as an EAM-worthy metric.   
5 
 
6 
The Companies believe that the Supply Diversification EAM metric is 
7 
consistent with the intent of the Track Two Order, which was to align 
8 
utility shareholders’ financial interests with customers’ interests.  As with 
9 
Staff’s recommended Peak Reduction EAM, which was not initially 
10 
included in the Track Two Order for gas EAMs, the Supply 
11 
Diversification EAM would incentivize the Companies to prioritize 
12 
actions that are not currently aligned with the Companies’ business model. 
13 
Although RNG can help the Companies address supply constraints, they 
14 
currently have no incentive to prioritize RNG interconnection over the 
15 
interconnection of traditional natural gas supply infrastructure or non-
16 
traditional natural gas supply infrastructure – e.g., CNG or LNG. The 
17 
Companies currently generate no profit from interconnecting third-party 
18 
RNG projects.  The Companies should be incentivized to prioritize RNG 
19 
interconnections, because of the unique benefits of RNG, including 
20 
emissions reductions and improved local waste management.

20

21 
200

Page 46 of 66 
 
A. 
The Companies disagree with Staff (SEAMP pp. 41-42, 47) that the 
1 
Affordability metric should be rejected.  First, the Companies believe that 
2 
the outcome-oriented design of the Affordability metric means that it 
3

4

5 
utility partnerships and platforms meant to encourage markets to reduce 
6

7 
priority for both the Companies and the State, this metric should be 
8 
adopted to promote deeper engagement by the market in this population 
9 
and to measure the ultimate impacts of those activities. 
10 
 
11

12 
direct testimony should be higher.  Therefore, the Companies propose to 
13 
adjust the targets for the Affordability metric from 0.2 percent to 1.0 
14 
percent reduction in the low-income customer bill.  That is, the Companies 
15 
propose to earn one basis point for each one percent reduction in the 
16 
average low-income customer bill.  Unlike other proposed EAM metrics, 
17 
the Companies have not proposed to earn prorated incentives for this 
18 
metric.

A. 
City of New York 
17 
1. 
Energy Efficiency 
18 
Q.

19 
(“City”) that the Companies increase their respective incremental 
20 
207


--- Page 208 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel

energy efficiency target savings based on lower per-unit costs for 
1

2

17

18 
incentivizing customers to pursue comprehensive energy efficiency

21

Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 54 of 66 
 
offer higher incentives when measures are combined, and implementing 
1 
changes to enable or improve access to private funding sources.   
2 
 
3 
The Companies are not opposed to evaluating other potentially effective 
4 
energy efficiency strategies, and believe the City identifies a number of 
5 
approaches worth considering.  However, these rates case are not the 
6 
appropriate forum for doing so.  Rather, a more appropriate forum to 
7

8

10

11 
analyses that to date have not be performed.  The Companies are open to 
12

13 
collaborative; however, not as part of these rate cases.

Sustainable Heat Initiative 
6 
Q.

7

8

9 
A.

10 
Demonstration project to assess potential utility ownership of geothermal 
11 
underground loop systems.  The City’s proposed study could further the 
12 
understandings of the feasibility and potential benefits of similar systems 
13 
in institutional settings such as colleges.  District-type systems can be 
14

15

16 
Companies have not estimated the costs to perform such studies, nor 
17 
budgeted for the associated costs of the studies in their revenue 
18 
requirements.

4. 
Performance-Based Incentives and Revenue Sharing 
1 
(EAMs) 
2 
Q. 
Please 
address 
the 
City’s 
recommendations 
regarding 
the 
3 
Companies’ proposed EAMs. 
4 
A. 
The City supports (NYCEEP pp. 30-42) the concept of EAMs, but 
5 
opposes specific EAMs proposed by the Companies.  The Companies 
6 
agree in principle that EAMs should be designed to provide increasing 
7 
levels of incentive for increasing levels of performance; however, we 
8 
disagree with the City that the EAMs proposed by the Companies are 
9 
inadequate or otherwise inappropriate.   
10 
 
11 
   
Regarding the System Efficiency (Peak Reduction) metric, the 
12 
Companies propose an increasing scale of incentives, where the 
13 
minimum incentive requires the Companies to achieve their target 
14 
performance.  If they do not achieve the targets, the EAM is not 
15 
triggered.  The potential to earn above the minimum requires incremental 
16 
effort and performance.  As described above in relation to the Staff’s 
17 
position on this metric, the Companies support the concept of developing 
18 
a more robust peak reduction metric design; however, until such a metric 
19 
is developed and can be implemented, the Commission should approve 
20 
the Companies’ proposed peak-reduction metric.     
21

1

4 
experience in 2017 and 2018.  For the reasons the Panel stated above

B. 
The Environmental Defense Fund (“EDF”) 
6

7 
A.

8

9 
broad-based GHG EAM (Ucar); (2) gas supply planning (Lander); and 
10 
(3) leak detection and repair (Von Fischer).  The Panel addresses item 
11 
(1), above.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe EDF’s recommendation regarding developing utility 
14 
GHG inventories.   
15 
A. 
EDF recommends (Ucar pp. 9-12) that gas utilities work with state and 
16 
local entities and electric utilities to gather economy-wide information to 
17 
establish a current, holistic GHG emissions status and baseline 
18 
attributable to the utility’s geographical footprint.  The Companies agree 
19 
that understanding the nature and extent of GHG sources is vital to 
20 
developing effective strategies to address emissions.  We also recognize, 
21 
215


--- Page 216 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 61 of 66 
 
however, that GHG sources exist in all sectors of the economy, and 
1 
utilities do not necessarily possess the resources necessary to develop 
2 
such economy-wide GHG inventories.  Although the Companies are 
3 
open to participating in collaborative efforts to arrive at appropriate GHG 
4 
inventories against which reductions can be assessed, we do not agree 
5 
that utilities should be responsible for developing economy-wide GHG 
6 
inventories as EDF proposes.   
7 
 
8 
 
Regarding EDF’s potential incentive for broad GHG emissions reductions, 
9 
the Companies agree that incentives to promote such reductions are 
10 
desirable; and in fact the Companies proposed two Carbon Reduction 
11 
metrics as part of its proposed package of EAMs in this case.

F. 
Sane Energy Project (“Sane”)  
8 
Q. 
Please address Sane’s recommendations.   
9 
A. 
Sane presents several positions and proposals in its testimony (Fraczek & 
10 
Ziesche).  The Company rejects Sane’s proposal that the Companies not 
11 
invest to address leak prone pipe (“LPP”).  As Witness Von Fischer of 
12 
EDF describes, addressing leaks from LPP is vital in reducing methane 
13 
leaks and addressing GHG emissions.  Sane also opposes the Companies’ 
14 
Clean Conversion Program; however, as the Panel explained above, the 
15 
Companies are not pursuing the program in light of the current moratoria 
16 
on new connections.   
17 
 
18 
 
Regarding Sane’s recommendations on LNG and CNG investments, the 
19 
Companies strongly disagree.  The proposed LNG and CNG investments 
20 
in the Companies’ capital plans are for projects to maintain the safety and 
21 
219


--- Page 220 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 65 of 66 
 
reliability of these critical facilities that are often needed to meet peak 
1 
demands.  Failure to adequately maintain the safety and integrity of these 
2 
facilities could jeopardize the ability of the Companies to meet the 
3 
energy needs of New Yorkers when they are most critical.   
4 
 
5 
 
With regard to radon testing, Sane points to study findings of increased 
6 
radon levels in buildings located near active hydraulic fracturing sites to 
7 
support its recommendation that the Companies provide radon 
8 
monitoring in all gas supplied to customers in New York City and Long 
9 
Island.  The Companies disagree.  There is no basis for concluding that 
10 
radon gas that may accumulate in buildings and basements proximate to 
11 
fracturing operations in Pennsylvania is in the gas delivered to customers 
12 
in New York.   
13 
 
14 
 
Finally, the Companies respectfully oppose Sane’s recommendation that 
15 
it receive intervenor funding.   
16 
G. 
Robert Mark Wyman (“Bob Wyman”)  
17 
Q. 
Please address the recommendations of Bob Wyman. 
18 
A. 
Bob Wyman recommends a number of changes in how utilities operate in 
19 
order to eliminate use of gas.  Bob Wyman’s proposed approach, however, 
20 
would likely cause substantial unintended disruption in the Companies’ 
21 
220


--- Page 221 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
Page 66 of 66 
 
ability to operate their networks to serve the energy needs of customers, 
1 
not to mention the potential for significant financial impacts.  Although he 
2 
may hold sincere views on what should be done to curb fossil fuel use and 
3 
reduce GHG emissions, Bob Wyman’s recommendations, if adopted, 
4 
would result in significant economic and operational disruption and would 
5 
be infeasible in the near term.  Accordingly, the Companies oppose the 
6 
recommendations in Mr. Wyman’s testimony.

III. 
NE:NY Order 
16 
Q. 
Please provide a summary of the NE:NY Order. 
17 
A. 
The NE:NY Order marks the next step in the State’s energy 
18 
transformation, driving significant market-enabling investments aimed at 
19 
reducing energy consumption and achieving ambitious clean energy 
20 
226


--- Page 227 ---
Supplemental Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
4 of 12 
 
 
goals.  It establishes progressive energy efficiency budgets and targets 
1 
for calendar years 2021 through 2025, directs resources to helping 
2 
disadvantaged communities and low- and moderate-income (“LMI”) 
3 
customers achieve energy savings, identifies strategies to address gas 
4 
supply-constrained areas, incentivizes the achievement of superior 
5 
energy efficiency outcomes, and provides a framework to implement, 
6 
review, and refine the overall approach to energy efficiency.  The 
7 
Companies share the State’s vision for the clean energy future.  To that 
8 
end, this supplemental testimony incorporates specific elements of the 
9 
shared vision, as set forth in the NE:NY Order. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What elements of the NE:NY Order impact the Panel’s Future of 
12 
Heat proposals? 
13 
A. 
Most notably, the Companies have updated the revenue requirements to 
14 
reflect the new energy efficiency budgets and targets set forth in the 
15 
NE:NY Order.  Furthermore, to deliver the benefits in the NE:NY Order 
16 
and meet the reporting and compliance requirements contained therein, 
17 
the Companies seek eight additional full-time equivalents (“FTEs”).  The 
18 
hiring of the FTEs will be staggered throughout the Rate Year and Data 
19 
Year 1, as discussed below.  The Companies also look to build on the 
20 
227


--- Page 228 ---
Supplemental Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
5 of 12 
 
 
earnings adjustment mechanism (“EAM”) framework to further 
1 
incentivize achieving superior energy efficiency outcomes.  Finally, the 
2 
Companies highlight ways in which the forward-looking Future of Heat 
3 
programs and services align with the “all cost-effective measures” 
4 
approach to delivering clean energy solutions set forth in the NE:NY 
5 
Order and embodied in the Climate Leadership and Community 
6 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”).  Each of these changes is described below 
7 
more fully. 
8 
 
9 
 
A. 
Budgets and Targets 
10 
Q. 
Please summarize the Companies’ energy efficiency budget proposal.  
11 
A. 
The Companies’ original rate filing used the presumptive targets and 
12 
budgets in the Commission’s December 13, 2018 “Order Adopting 
13 
Accelerated 
Energy 
Efficiency 
Targets” 
in 
Case 
18-M-0084 
14 
(“Accelerated Efficiency Order”) to set the proposed Rate Year energy 
15 
efficiency rate allowance.  With the NE:NY Order, the Companies have 
16 
incorporated the updated energy efficiency budgets for the Rate Year, 
17 
which represents an incremental increase of $1.232 million for KEDLI 
18 
and $0.070 million for KEDNY, as compared to the presumptive budgets 
19 
228


--- Page 229 ---
Supplemental Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
6 of 12 
 
 
from the Accelerated Efficiency Order.  Please see Exhibit __ (RRP-
1 
1TS) for further detail on the revenue requirement adjustments. 
2 
  
3 
Q. 
How does the NE:NY Order impact the Companies’ proposal to 
4 
treat EM&V costs separately from the energy efficiency budget?  
5 
A. 
The NE:NY Order clarifies that the authorized energy efficiency budgets 
6 
include EM&V costs.  Accordingly, the Companies have adjusted their 
7 
respective revenue requirement to remove EM&V costs from base rates 
8 
and instead incorporate those costs in their energy efficiency program 
9 
portfolio budgets. 
10 
 
 
 
11 
Q. 
Will the Companies require additional FTEs to support the NE:NY 
12 
Order?  
13 
A. 
Yes.  The NE:NY Order ramps up the energy efficiency targets by 114 
14 
percent and 22 percent for KEDLI and KEDNY, respectively, for 2021 
15 
through 2025. As set forth in Exhibit __ (RRP-2TS), the Companies 
16 
request eight FTEs in addition to the seven FTEs originally requested in 
17 
the Companies’ initial filing.  The new positions are needed to enable the

B. 
Incentive Mechanisms 
15 
Q. 
Please address the impacts of the revised energy efficiency targets in 
16 
the NE:NY Order on the Companies’ EAM proposal.  
17 
A. 
The NE:NY Order defers to rate proceedings for the development and 
18 
refinement of EAM proposals while maintaining elements of statewide 
19 
uniformity with the incentives.  To that end, the Companies will use the 
20 
232


--- Page 233 ---
Supplemental Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
10 of 12 
 
 
energy efficiency targets established in the NE:NY Order as the basis for 
1 
the minimum threshold for their respective energy efficiency EAMs. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed changes to KEDLI’s 
4 
EmPower Replacement Program Incentive Mechanism.  
5 
A. 
As set forth in the Third Supplemental Testimony of the Revenue 
6 
Requirements Panel, KEDLI supports incorporating the HEAT Program 
7 
into its energy efficiency portfolio. Given the incremental funding 
8 
authorized for LMI energy efficiency programs in the NE:NY Order, 
9 
KEDLI no longer believes it is necessary to request additional funding for 
10 
the program outside of the energy efficiency budget. Instead, the 
11 
Companies will fund this program within the authorized energy efficiency 
12 
portfolio budgets and the Companies will make an adjustment to the 
13 
revenue requirement to remove the incremental $2.5 million. The 
14 
Companies still propose that the Earnings Incentive Mechanism be applied 
15 
to this program as discussed in the Panel’s rebuttal testimony. 
16 
 
17

19

A.

1

2 
a focus on gas peak reduction in supply-constrained areas, while also 
3 
highlighting the importance of heat pumps, even calling out the need for 
4

5 
pump deployment in KEDNY’s service territory. 
6 
 
7 
The Companies developed a suite of proposals in the Future of Heat 
8 
filing that align with the NE:NY Order’s clean energy focus, as well as 
9 
the broader CLCPA clean energy goals.  Specifically, the Companies’ 
10 
proposed fuel-switching calculator is a customer-facing technology 
11 
intended to reduce barriers to information, facilitate the further 
12 
development of energy efficiency markets, and accelerate the adoption of 
13 
energy efficiency technologies.  Likewise, the Companies’ expanded 
14 
geothermal and demand reduction demonstration projects address the 
15 
precise issues raised in the NE:NY Order: supply constraints and 
16 
building electrification through heat pump deployment.  The Companies 
17 
continue to believe that these demonstration projects can play an 
18

19-G-0309/19-G-0310 - 2-10-20 - Brooklyn Union Gas 
-- talk about monetizing the environmental attributes 
of R.N.G. 
Currently, today, R.N.G. is mostly 
being utilized in the transportation sector under 
E.P.A's R.F.S. program, renewable fuel standards, and 
it generates something known as R.I.N.S., Renewable 
Identification Numbers. 
So if you use R.N.G. in the 
transportation sector, you generate R.I.N.S. and they 
have market value.  So this really refers to selling 
R.N.G. into the transportation sector and 
specifically within the scope of the Green Gas Tariff 
proposal. 
The idea here would be to allow some 
mechanism such that if the company had excess gas as 
referred to in line 15 on page 32 that the company 
would be able to -- to do something with that volume 
and to the extent that there was a positive financial 
benefit from that, a revenue stream effectively from 
monetizing the environmental attributes of the R.N.G. 
That cost would then go back into the 
-- the program cost at large to reduce the R.N.G. 
costs for participating Green Gas Tariff customers.
